. But then, by definition and the fact that
y\in\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}\backslash\{0\}
, we must have

0=\sum^{r}_{i=1}(\bm{z}_{1}-\bm{z}_{2})_{i}N_{\bm{e}_{i}}=(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})\sum^{r}_{i=1}z_{i}N_{\bm{e}_{i}}.

Suppose that
\theta_{1}>\theta_{2}
without loss of generality and let
\bm{x}\in D
be the vector
(\mathrm{sign}(z_{i}))^{\infty}_{i=1}
. Then,
\Psi_{\bm{e}_{i}}(\bm{x})=\sqrt{3}\mathrm{sign}(z_{i})
and therefore

0\geq(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})\left(\sqrt{3}{\|\bm{z}\|}_{1}-\delta r\right)\geq(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})\left(\sqrt{3}{\|\bm{z}\|}_{2}-\delta r\right)=(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})\left(\sqrt{3}{\|\bm{z}\|}_{2}-2^{-m}/r\right),

since
{\|N_{\bm{e}_{i}}-\Psi_{\bm{e}_{i}}\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\varrho}(D)}\leq\delta
and
\delta\leq 2^{-m}/r^{2}
by definition. We now choose
\bm{z}
with
{\|\bm{z}\|}_{2}=2^{-m}/r\leq 2^{-m}
. Note that this choice of
\bm{z}
does not change the error bound, except for a constant. It also yields

0\geq(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})(\sqrt{3}2^{-m}-2^{-m})>0

which is a contradiction. Hence
\widehat{N}_{1}\neq\widehat{N}_{2}
. Thus, we have shown that any
\theta\in[-1,1]
leads to a distinct DNN minimizer that satisfies the desired bounds. We get the result.

Step 7: Modifications when
\mathcal{Y}
is a Hilbert space.
The modifications required when
\mathcal{Y}
is a Hilbert space are identical to those needed in the proof of Theorem
3.1
(see §
D.8
). We omit the details.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.2
, Statement (B).

Let
N=N_{\bm{\theta}}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}
be a tanh DNN, where
\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
are the network parameters (weights and biases). Let
{\|\cdot\|}_{(d_{\mathcal{X}})}
,
{\|\cdot\|}_{(d_{\mathcal{Y}})}
and
{\|\cdot\|}_{(D)}
be arbitrary norms on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}
,
\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}
and
\mathbb{R}^{D}
, respectively. Then, since the activation function is a Lipschitz function, we have

{\|N_{\bm{\theta}^{\prime}}(\bm{x})-N_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x})\|}_{(d_{\mathcal{Y}})}\leq c_{\bm{\theta}}{\|\bm{\theta}^{\prime}-\bm{\theta}\|}_{(D)}({\|\bm{x}\|}_{(d_{\mathcal{X}})}+1),

where
c_{\bm{\theta}}>0
is a constant depending on
\bm{\theta}
.

Now let
\widehat{N}=\widehat{N}_{\hat{\bm{\theta}}}
and
\widehat{F}=\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}\circ\widehat{N}\circ\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{X}}
and consider
F=\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}\circ N\circ\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{X}}
for
N=N_{\bm{\theta}}
.
Since
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is linear and therefore bounded, we have

{\|\widehat{F}(X)-F(X)\|}_{\mathcal{Y}}\leq c_{\hat{\bm{\theta}}}{\|\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|}_{(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}},{\|\cdot\|}_{(d_{\mathcal{Y}})})\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}}{\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}-\bm{\theta}\|}_{(D)}\left({\|\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{X}}(X)\|}_{(d_{\mathcal{X}})}+1\right).

We deduce that

\displaystyle{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{F}-F|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{L^{2}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}

\displaystyle\leq{\|\widehat{F}-F\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\hat{\bm{\theta}}}{\|\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}\|}_{(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}},{\|\cdot\|}_{(d_{\mathcal{Y}})})\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}}{\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}-\bm{\theta}\|}_{(D)}\left({\|\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{X}}\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{X}}},{\|\cdot\|}_{(d_{\mathcal{X}})})}+1\right).

Therefore, there exists a neighbourhood around
\hat{\bm{\theta}}
for which

{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{F}-F|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{L^{2}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}\leq{\|\widehat{F}-F\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}\leq\tau_{o}

for all parameters
\bm{\theta}
in the neighbourhood. The result now follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.2
, Statement (C).

By construction, the DNN
\widehat{N}
defined in (
E.4
) contains subnetworks that compute the DNNs
N_{\bm{e}_{i}}
,
i=1,\ldots,r
, which themselves are approximations to the Legendre polynomials
\Psi_{\bm{e}_{i}}
. The construction of these subnetworks was described in the proof of Lemma
D.9
as the composition of an affine map defined by the fundamental theorem of algebra and a tanh DNN that approximately multiplies
m(\Gamma)
numbers. In this specific case, we have

\Psi_{\bm{e}_{i}}(\bm{x})=x_{i}.

Therefore, the corresponding affine map (
D.18
)
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{e}_{i}}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m(\Gamma)}
has a bias vector that is all ones, except for a single entry that has value zero. We deduce that the bias vector
\bm{b}
of the full DNN (
E.4
) contains a subblock of size
rm(\Gamma)
that is all ones, except for
r
zeroes. There are
{rm(\Gamma)\choose r}
rearrangements of this subblock, each of which leading to a bias vector
\bm{b}^{\prime}
with
{\|\bm{b}-\bm{b}^{\prime}\|}\gtrsim 1
. Moreover,
\bm{b}^{\prime}
leads to the same DNN, after permuting the various weight matrices in the corresponding way. Indeed, if
\bm{P}
is a permutation matrix, then
\sigma(\bm{W}\bm{x}+\bm{b})=\bm{P}^{-1}\sigma(\bm{P}\bm{W}\bm{x}+\bm{P}\bm{b})
, since
\sigma
acts componentwise.

It remains to bound
{rm(\Gamma)\choose r}
from below. We have

{rm(\Gamma)\choose r}\geq\frac{(rm(\Gamma))^{r}}{r^{r}}=m(\Gamma)^{r}.

By (
E.13
), we have that
r\geq 2m
. Now, by the definition (
E.1
) of
\Gamma
,

m(\Gamma)\geq\max_{S\in\mathcal{S}}m(S),

where
\mathcal{S}
is as in (
D.1
) for
k
as in (
E.12
) and
\Lambda=\Lambda^{\mathsf{HCI}}_{n}
as in (
D.26
) with
n
as in (
D.29
). Now consider the set
S=\{l\bm{e}_{1}\}
for some
l\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

|S|_{\bm{v}}=v^{2}_{\bm{\nu}}=(2l+1)^{5+\xi}=(2l+1)^{1/\delta}.

Therefore,
S\in\mathcal{S}
provided
(2l+1)^{1/\delta}\leq k
and
l\leq n
. Set
l=\lfloor(k^{\delta}-1)/2\rfloor
and observe that
l\leq n
since
k\leq n
and
\delta\leq 1/5
by assumption. Therefore
S\in\mathcal{S}
. Using the definition of
k
, we get that

m(\Gamma)\geq m(S)=l\geq(m/(c_{4}L))^{\delta}

for all sufficiently large
m
. The result now follows.
∎

Appendix F
Proof of Theorem
4.1

The proof of Theorem
4.1
will follow as a consequence of the following result. For this, we require the following notation. Given
0<p\leq\infty
,
s\in\mathbb{N}
and a sequence
\bm{c}=(c_{i})^{\infty}_{i=1}
, we let

\sigma_{s}(\bm{c})_{p}=\min\{{\|\bm{c}-\bm{z}\|}_{p}:\bm{z}\in\ell^{2},|\mathrm{supp}(\bm{z})|\leq s\},

where
\mathrm{supp}(\bm{z})=\{i\in\mathbb{N}:z_{i}\neq 0\}
for
\bm{z}=(z_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Theorem F.1
.

For any
0<p<1
then term
\theta_{m}(\bm{b})
defined in (
4.2
) satisfies

\theta_{m}(\bm{b})\gtrsim\sigma_{m}(\bm{b})_{2},\quad\forall\bm{b}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{N}),\ \bm{b}\geq\bm{0},{\|\bm{b}\|}_{1}\leq 1.

As noted, the proof of this theorem is based on
[
7
, Thm. 4.4]
. We recap the details as they will also be needed in the proof of the next result. First, we recall some basic definitions. See
[
82
]
or
[
29
, Ch. 10]
for more details. Let
\mathcal{K}
be a subset of a normed space
(\mathcal{X},{\|\cdot\|}_{\mathcal{X}})
. Then its
Gelfand
m
-width
is

d^{m}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{X})=\inf\left\{\sup_{x\in\mathcal{K}\cap L^{m}}{\|x\|}_{\mathcal{X}},\ \text{$L^{m}$ a subspace of $\mathcal{X}$ with $\mathrm{codim}(L^{m})\leq m$}\right\}.

(F.1)

An equivalent representation is

d^{m}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{X})=\inf\left\{\sup_{x\in\mathcal{K}\cap\mathrm{Ker}(A)}{\|x\|}_{\mathcal{X}},\ A:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}\text{ linear}\right\}.

The Gelfand width is related to the following quantity:

E^{m}_{\mathsf{ada}}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{X})=\inf\left\{\sup_{x\in\mathcal{K}}{\|x-\Delta(\Gamma(x))\|}_{\mathcal{X}},\ \Gamma:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}\text{ adaptive},\ \Delta:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}\right\},

(F.2)

where
\Delta
is an arbitrary (potentially nonlinear) reconstruction map and
\Gamma
is an
adaptive
sampling map. By this, we mean that

\Gamma(x)=\begin{bmatrix}\Gamma_{1}(x)\\
\Gamma_{2}(x,\Gamma_{1}(x))\\
\vdots\\
\Gamma_{m}(x,\Gamma_{1}(x),\ldots,\Gamma_{m-1}(x))\end{bmatrix},

where
\Gamma_{1}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is linear and, for
i=2,\ldots,m
,
\Gamma_{i}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{i-1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is linear in its first component.

Proof of Theorem
F.1
.

We proceed in a series of steps.

Step 1: Setup.

Define the functions

\phi_{i}(\bm{x})=\sqrt{3}x_{i},\quad\bm{x}\in D,\qquad i=1,2,\ldots.

Notice that these functions form an orthonormal system in
L^{2}_{\varrho}(D)
. (A.I) implies that these functions form a Riesz system in
L^{2}_{\varsigma}(D)
, with Riesz constants that are
\asymp 1
. Hence they have a (unique) biorthogonal Riesz system
\{\psi_{i}\}^{\infty}_{i=1}\subset L^{2}_{\varsigma}(D)
.
Now define
\Phi_{i}=\phi_{i}\circ\iota
and
\Psi_{i}=\psi_{i}\circ\iota
. Let
G\in L^{2}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})
be arbitrary. Then

{\|G\|}_{L^{2}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})}\geq\frac{\langle G,\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}\langle G,\Psi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})}\Psi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})}}{{\|\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}\langle G,\Psi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})}\Psi_{i}\|}_{L^{2}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})}}=\frac{\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}|\langle G,\Psi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})}|^{2}}{{\|\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}\langle G,\Psi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})}\Psi_{i}\|}_{L^{2}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})}}.

Consider the denominator. Using (A.II) and fact that the
\psi_{i}
form a Riesz system, we see that

\displaystyle{\left\|\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}\langle G,\Psi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})}\Psi_{i}\right\|}_{L^{2}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle={\left\|\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}\langle G,\Psi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})}\psi_{i}\right\|}_{L^{2}_{\varsigma}(D)}\lesssim\sqrt{\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}|\langle G,\Psi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})}|^{2}}.

We deduce that

{\|G\|}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})}\gtrsim\sqrt{\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}|\langle G,\Psi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})}|^{2}},\quad\forall G\in L^{2}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R}).

(F.3)

Now let
\bm{b}\geq\bm{0}
with
\bm{b}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{N})
and
I\subset\mathbb{N}
with
|I|=N
. Using
[
7
, Lem. 5.2]
we see that the function

f=c\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}y\phi_{i}\in\mathcal{H}(\bm{b}),

(F.4)

for any
y\in\mathcal{Y}
,
{\|y\|}_{\mathcal{Y}}=1
and
\bm{c}=(c_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
with
|\bm{c}|\leq\bm{b}
(i.e.,
|c_{i}|\leq b_{i}
,
\forall i
), where
c>0
is a universal constant.

Step 2: Reduction to a discrete problem.

Let
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{R}
be arbitrary sampling and reconstruction maps as in (
4.2
). Following
[
7
, Lem. 5.3]
, let
F=f\circ\iota
and observe that

F(X)=cy\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}\Phi_{i}(X)

and therefore

\mathcal{L}(F)=y\Gamma(\bm{c}),

where
\Gamma:\mathbb{R}^{|I|}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
is given by

\Gamma(\bm{z})=\begin{bmatrix}c\sum_{i\in I}z_{i}\Phi_{i}(X_{1})\\
\vdots\\
c\sum_{i\in I}z_{i}\Phi_{i}(X_{m})\end{bmatrix},

due to (
4.1
). Notice that
\Gamma
is an adaptive sampling map of the form defined above. Now let
y^{*}\in B(\mathcal{Y}^{*})
be such that
|y^{*}(y)|={\|y\|}_{\mathcal{Y}}
. Then, by (
F.3
),

\displaystyle{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|F-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{L}(F)|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}

\displaystyle\geq{\|y^{*}(F-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{L}(F))\|}^{2}_{L^{2}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\sum_{i\in I}|\langle y^{*}(F-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{L}(F)),\Psi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})}|^{2}.

By biorthogonality,
\langle y^{*}(F),\Psi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})}=c{\|y\|}_{\mathcal{Y}}c_{i}=c\cdot c_{i}
,
which implies that

{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|F-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{L}(F)|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{L^{2}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}\gtrsim{\|\bm{c}-\Delta\circ\Gamma(\bm{c})\|}_{2},

(F.5)

where

\Delta:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N},\quad\bm{y}\mapsto\Delta(\bm{y})=\left(\langle y^{*}(\mathcal{R}(y\cdot\bm{y})),\Psi_{i}\rangle_{L^{2}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{R})}/c\right)_{i\in I}.

Therefore,

\sup_{F\in\mathcal{H}(\bm{b},\iota)}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|F-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{L}(F)|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{L^{2}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}\gtrsim\inf_{\Gamma,\Delta}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}\\
\mathrm{supp}(\bm{c})\subseteq I\\
|\bm{c}|\leq\bm{b}\end{subarray}}{\|\bm{c}-\Delta\circ\Gamma(\bm{c})\|}_{2},

where the infimum is taken over all adaptive sampling maps
\Gamma
and reconstruction maps
\Delta
. Since
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{R}
were arbitrary, we get

\theta_{m}(\bm{b})\gtrsim E^{\mathsf{ada}}_{m}(B(\bm{b},I),\ell^{2}_{N}),

where
B(\bm{b},I)=\{\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{|I|}:|z_{i}|\leq b_{i},\ i\in I\}
and
\ell^{2}_{N}=(\mathbb{R}^{N},{\|\cdot\|}_{2})
.

Step 3: Derivation of the lower bounds.
The next step is identical to the proof of Theorem 4.4 in
[
7
]
. This gives (
F.1
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

We use Theorem
F.1
.
For (i), we let
\bm{b}=(b_{i})^{\infty}_{i=1}
by defined by

b_{i}=(2m)^{-1/p},\ i=1,\ldots,2m,\qquad b_{i}=0,\ i>2m.

This sequence
\bm{b}\in\ell^{p}_{\mathsf{M}}(\mathbb{N})
with
{\|\bm{b}\|}_{p,\mathsf{M}}={\|\bm{b}\|}_{p}=1
. Moreover, we have

\sigma_{m}(\bm{b})_{2}=2^{-1/p}m^{1/2-1/p}.

For (ii) we let
\bm{b}=(b_{i})^{\infty}_{i=1}
be defined by
b_{i}=c_{p}(i\log^{2}(i))^{-1/p}
, where
c_{p}=\left(\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}1/(i\log^{2}(i))\right)^{-1/p}
. This sequence
\bm{b}\in\ell^{p}_{\mathsf{M}}(\mathbb{N})
with
{\|\bm{b}\|}_{p,\mathsf{M}}={\|\bm{b}\|}_{p}=1
. Moreover,

\sigma_{m}(\bm{b})^{2}_{2}\geq c^{2}_{p}\sum^{2m}_{i=m+1}(i\log^{2}(i))^{-2/p}\geq c^{2}_{p}\cdot m\cdot(2m\log^{2}(2m))^{-2/p},

as required.
∎

Appendix G
Proof of Theorem
4.2

Much as in the previous section, the proof of Theorem
4.2
is a consequence of the following result.

Theorem G.1
.

Suppose that the pushforward
\varsigma
in (A.I) is a tensor-product of a univariate probability measure. Then the term
\tilde{\theta}_{m}(\bm{b})
defined in (
4.3
) satisfies

\tilde{\theta}_{m}(\bm{b})\gtrsim\sigma_{m}(\bm{b})_{1}/\log(m),\quad\forall\bm{b}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{N}),\ \bm{b}\geq\bm{0},{\|\bm{b}\|}_{1}\leq 1.

Proof of Theorem
G.1
.

We proceed in a similar series of steps to those of the last proof.

Step 1: Setup.

Let
\pi:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
be a bijection that gives a nonincreasing rearrangement of
\bm{b}
, i.e.,
b_{\pi(1)}\geq b_{\pi(2)}\geq\cdots
. Now, let
r\in\mathbb{N}
be arbitrary and consider the index set

I=I_{1}\cup\cdots\cup I_{r},\quad I_{l}=\{\pi((l-1)(m+1)+1),\ldots,\pi(l(m+1))\}.

Notice that
|I|=r(m+1)
.
Define the matrix

\bm{A}=\left((\iota(X_{i}))_{\pi(j)}\right)^{m,r(m+1)}_{i,j=1}

and notice that we may write

\bm{A}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{A}_{1}&\cdots&\bm{A}_{r}\end{bmatrix},\quad\text{where }\bm{A}_{l}=\left((\iota(X_{i}))_{\pi((l-1)(m+1)+j)}\right)^{m,m+1}_{i,j=1}.

Let
\sigma
be the one-dimensional probability measure associated with
\varsigma
. Then notice that each
\bm{A}_{l}
is a random matrix whose entries are drawn i.i.d. from
\sigma
. Since
\sigma
is supported in
[-1,1]
, we deduce that the
\bm{A}_{l}
are independent subgaussian random matrices with the same distribution. Write
\gamma
for this distribution. Let
t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}>0
and
E_{l,t_{l}}
be the event

E_{l,t_{l}}=\left\{\exists\bm{u}\in N(\bm{A}_{l}):{\|\bm{u}\|}_{2}=1,\ {\|\bm{u}\|}_{\infty}<\sqrt{t_{l}/(m+1)}\right\},\quad l=1,\ldots,r.

(G.1)

We will make a suitable choice of
t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}
later.

Step 2: Reduction to a discrete problem.
Let

\mathcal{C}=\left\{\bm{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{|I|}:|c_{i}|\leq b_{i},\ \forall i\in I,\ \bm{c}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{c}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{c}_{r}\end{bmatrix},\bm{c}_{l}\in N(\bm{A}_{l}),\ l=1,\ldots,r\right\}.

Notice that any
\bm{c}\in\mathcal{C}
also satisfies
\bm{c}\in N(\bm{A})
.
Now let
f=f_{\bm{c}}
be as in (
F.4
) (we make the dependence on
\bm{c}
explicit now for convenience).
Let
\bm{x}\in D
. Then

f_{\bm{c}}(\bm{x})=\sqrt{3}cy\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}x_{i}.

(G.2)

We deduce that

\left(F_{\bm{c}}(X_{i})\right)^{m}_{i=1}=\sqrt{3}cy\bm{A}\bm{c}=\bm{0},

where
F_{\bm{c}}=f_{\bm{c}}\circ\iota
. This implies that

{\|F-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{L}(F)\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}={\|F-\mathcal{R}(\{X_{i},0\}^{m}_{i=1})\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})},

where, for convenience, we let
\mathcal{L}:F\mapsto\{X_{i},F(X_{i})\}^{m}_{i=1}
.
Therefore,

\sup_{F\in\mathcal{H}(\bm{b},\iota)}{\|F-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{L}(F)\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}\geq\sup_{\bm{c}\in\mathcal{C}}{\|F_{\bm{c}}-\mathcal{R}(\{X_{i},0\}^{m}_{i=1})\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}.

Now observe that
F_{\bm{0}}=0
and
\bm{0}\in\mathcal{C}
. Hence

\displaystyle\sup_{F\in\mathcal{H}(\bm{b},\iota)}

\displaystyle{\|F-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{L}(F)\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}

\displaystyle\geq\max\left\{{\|\mathcal{R}(\{X_{i},0\}^{m}_{i=1})\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})},\sup_{\bm{c}\in\mathcal{C}}{\|F_{\bm{c}}\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}-{\|\mathcal{R}(\{X_{i},0\}^{m}_{i=1})\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}\right\}.

For
a>0
, the function
x\mapsto\max\{x,a-x\}
is minimized at
x=a/2
and takes value
a/2
there. We deduce that

\sup_{F\in\mathcal{H}(\bm{b},\iota)}{\|F-\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{L}(F)\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}\geq\frac{1}{2}\sup_{\bm{c}\in\mathcal{C}}{\|F_{\bm{c}}\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}.

Now, by (A.I),

{\|F_{\bm{c}}\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}={\|f_{\bm{c}}\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\varsigma}(D;\mathcal{Y})}\gtrsim{\|f_{\bm{c}}\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\varrho}(D;\mathcal{Y})}=\sqrt{3}c\sup_{{\|\bm{x}\|}_{\infty}\leq 1}\left|\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}x_{i}\right|=\sqrt{3}c{\|\bm{c}\|}_{1}.

With this in hand, we conclude that

\mathbb{E}_{X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}\sim\mu}\sup_{F\in\mathcal{H}(\bm{b},\iota)}{\|F-\mathcal{R}(\{X_{i},F(X_{i})\}^{m}_{i=1})\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}\gtrsim\mathbb{E}_{\bm{A}_{1},\ldots,\bm{A}_{r}\sim\gamma}\sup_{\bm{c}\in\mathcal{C}}{\|\bm{c}\|}_{1}.

We now use the definition of
\mathcal{C}
to write

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}\sim\mu}\sup_{F\in\mathcal{H}(\bm{b},\iota)}&{\|F-\mathcal{R}(\{X_{i},F(X_{i})\}^{m}_{i=1})\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}\\
&\gtrsim\sum^{r}_{l=1}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{A}_{l}\sim\gamma}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{c}_{l}\in N(\bm{A}_{l})\\
|(\bm{c}_{l})_{i}|\leq b_{i},\forall i\in I_{l}\end{subarray}}{\|\bm{c}_{l}\|}_{1}.\end{split}

(G.3)

Step 3: Bounding the expected error.
Fix
l=1,\ldots,r
and suppose the event
E_{l,t_{l}}
defined (
G.1
) occurs. Let
\bm{u}_{l}
be the corresponding vector and define

\bm{c}_{l}=\frac{b_{\pi(l(m+1))}}{{\|\bm{u}_{l}\|}_{\infty}}\bm{u}_{l},\ l=1,\ldots,r.

By construction, we have that
\bm{c}_{l}\in N(\bm{A}_{l})
and
|(\bm{c}_{l})_{i}|\leq b_{i}
,
\forall i\in I_{l}
. We deduce that

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{c}_{l}\in N(\bm{A}_{l})\\
|(\bm{c}_{l})_{i}|\leq b_{i},\forall i\in I_{l}\end{subarray}}{\|\bm{c}_{l}\|}_{1}\geq\frac{b_{\pi(l(m+1))}{\|\bm{u}_{l}\|}_{1}}{{\|\bm{u}_{l}\|}_{\infty}}.

Now observe that

1={\|\bm{u}_{l}\|}^{2}_{2}\leq{\|\bm{u}_{l}\|}_{1}{\|\bm{u}_{l}\|}_{\infty}.

Therefore, we get that

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{c}_{l}\in N(\bm{A}_{l})\\
|(\bm{c}_{l})_{i}|\leq b_{i},\forall i\in I_{l}\end{subarray}}{\|\bm{c}_{l}\|}_{1}\geq\frac{b_{\pi(l(m+1))}}{{\|\bm{u}_{l}\|}^{2}_{\infty}}\geq\frac{b_{\pi(l(m+1))}(m+1)}{t_{l}}

whenever the event
E_{l,t_{l}}
occurs. Using the law of total expectation, we deduce that

\mathbb{E}_{\bm{A}_{l}\sim\gamma}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{c}_{l}\in N(\bm{A}_{l})\\
|(\bm{c}_{l})_{i}|\leq b_{i},\forall i\in I_{l}\end{subarray}}{\|\bm{c}_{l}\|}_{1}\geq\frac{b_{\pi(l(m+1))}(m+1)}{t_{l}}\mathbb{P}(E_{l,t_{l}})

for any fixed
t_{l}>0
.
We now appeal to
[
75
, Thm. 1.4]
. This shows that

\mathbb{P}(E^{c}_{l,t_{l}})\leq c_{2}m^{2}\exp(-t_{l}/c_{2}),\quad\forall t_{l}\geq c_{1}\log(m+1),

where
c_{1},c_{2}>0
are universal constants. We may without loss of generality assume that
c_{2}\geq c_{1}\geq 1
. Now set
t_{l}=c_{2}\log(2c_{2}m^{2})\geq c_{1}\log(m+1)
. Hence

\mathbb{P}(E^{c}_{l,t_{l}})\leq 1/2.

We deduce that
\mathbb{P}(E_{t,t_{l}})>1/2
and therefore

\mathbb{E}_{\bm{A}_{l}\sim\gamma}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{c}_{l}\in N(\bm{A}_{l})\\
|(\bm{c}_{l})_{i}|\leq b_{i},\forall i\in I_{l}\end{subarray}}{\|\bm{c}_{l}\|}_{1}\geq\frac{b_{\pi(l(m+1))}(m+1)}{2c_{2}\log(2c_{2}m^{2})}.

Now observe that

b_{\pi(l(m+1))}(m+1)\geq b_{\pi(l(m+1))}+\cdots+b_{\pi(l(m+1)+m)}

Substituting this into (
G.3
), we deduce that

\mathbb{E}_{X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}\sim\mu}\sup_{F\in\mathcal{H}(\bm{b},\iota)}{\|F-\mathcal{R}(\{X_{i},F(X_{i})\}^{m}_{i=1})\|}_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}(\mathcal{X};\mathcal{Y})}\gtrsim\frac{1}{\log(2m)}\sum^{r(m+1)+m}_{i=m+1}b_{\pi(i)}.

Since
r
was arbitrary, we may take the limit
r\rightarrow\infty
. We now use the fact that

\sigma_{m}(\bm{b})_{1}=b_{\pi(m+1)}+b_{\pi(m+2)}+\cdots.

to obtain the result.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

Using Theorem
G.1
, statements (i) and (ii) are derived in exactly the same way as in the proof of Theorem
4.1

∎
